When an occasion has become fully definite, Whitehead calls its achievement satisfaction, from satis and facere, to make enough, and his paired concept of subject-superject names its two offices: the occasion is the subject of its own becoming and, once achieved, a superject that other occasions can receive, passing into what he calls objective immortality as a condition of further becoming. A judgment has an everyday version of this afterlife: once I have made it, I can remember it, quote it, act on it, or find that someone has organised part of their life around it, and if I later change my mind, the earlier judgment has already entered the circumstances in which the change occurs.

We have just undergone a small concrescence of our own. The librarian's catalogue at first seemed clear enough to compile; then its two answers became incompatible demands; and understanding the paradox meant gathering the instruction, the attempted answers and their contradiction into one account in which Frege's principle stood exposed. Many steps became one understood construction, and that understanding is now a datum from which our next act of reading proceeds. We therefore propose the 4+2 as a logic of epistemic concrescence. Our definition of the Subject passed through four determinate positions, which recognition gathered as one performed inquiry, and its return through the implicate condition let the achieved understanding enter another act; written 0/1→4+2, the relation unfolds into an account that includes the differences through which it became intelligible. Whatever we identify along the way belongs to the articulated 1, yet its appearing and its relations keep returning us toward the 0 it cannot present as another object, and the slash holds that return in the inscription. In the sixfold the process takes an inspectable form, and 5→0, the Möbius return, marks the turn by which its completion becomes a beginning again. An answer reaches satisfaction when it is definite enough to contribute to further inquiry; its claim to be the last possible answer is a separate demand, and one that would arrest the process that made it knowable.

Whitehead's distinction between an occasion's formation and its reception by later occasions brings time into the account, since something becomes one and its achievement then enters a succession. Spencer-Brown approached the same question from the side the theory of types had closed. Where types forbid a form to re-enter itself, his Laws of Form lets a distinction re-enter the space it distinguishes and follows the consequence: an expression that contains itself has no stable value, it alternates between the marked and the unmarked state, and Spencer-Brown treats that alternation as the point at which time enters the calculus, the form's own way of holding what no single value can.88 That alternation is the clean oscillation of a held polarity, the healthy (−1)/(+1) given time to move in, where the decrees of Russell's swing had frozen it at +2 or −2. His re-entering mark writes, in a calculus of indications, the turn our notation writes as 5→0, the completed form returning into the space of its own drawing. Our 0/1 begins to articulate the logically prior, non-dual foundation of that paradoxical return: a reality that is its own act of self-identification.

Kashmir Śaivism thinks this pulse through in the Spanda school, named for the Spandakārikā, the ninth-century Stanzas on Vibration, which teach that consciousness is never inert: its spanda, its vibration or pulse, is a "subtle movement" that goes nowhere and yet manifests as every motion, the oscillation between the unchanging and the changeful, what Whitehead may have termed the primordial and consequent natures of the divine.89 Mark Dyczkowski, whose Doctrine of Vibration reconstructs the school's teaching from the Stanzas and their commentators Kallaṭa and Kṣemarāja, sets out the rhythm of a single act of that pulse through three powers of Śiva's consciousness: will (icchā), knowledge (jñāna) and action (kriyā). Everything comes into being through an act of will, with action as its immediate instrument and knowledge of how the will is to be applied mediating between the will to create and the act itself, so that the intent to make an object manifest, the act of manifesting and the manifest result are the beginning, middle and end of every experience.90 Read through our notation, that act is a 0/1. Will rises from consciousness at rest in itself, the 0 turning toward a determination; knowledge is the slash, the mediating awareness through which the will finds its application; action is the 1, the object or deed completed. Dyczkowski adds the return: the first moment of the will's intent coincides with its final moment, when the desire to act has been satisfied and consciousness rests again in pure intent, so the completed act turns back into the source from which the next act rises, and that is our 5→0. The comparison with concrescence holds at a technical level, for Whitehead's occasion also begins from an aim, integrates what it receives through its prehensions, and ends in a satisfaction that becomes a datum for what follows; Spanda places the whole rhythm in the knowing Subject, where, in Dyczkowski's words, "knowledge turns into action and action leads to knowledge".

Something also draws the inquiry onward before its answer is available. Russell's desire for certainty has already made that involvement explicit: the work mattered to him, and what he hoped to find helped determine what he was prepared to undertake. We call this participation of desire the eros of Logos. Jung gives the conjunction an exact psychological formulation in Mysterium Coniunctionis: "For purely psychological reasons I have, in other of my writings, tried to equate the masculine consciousness with the concept of Logos and the feminine with that of Eros. By Logos I meant discrimination, judgment, insight, and by Eros I meant the capacity to relate."91 The pair describes two operations which our own inquiry has repeatedly had to perform together. Logos cuts sufficiently cleanly for a difference to become intelligible; Eros keeps what has been distinguished in a relation through which the difference can matter. The syn-ballein of the symbolon already gave this relational work a material image: its halves have to remain distinct for their fitting to disclose their prior belonging. The eros of Logos is therefore the desire of articulation to recover and deepen the relations its own discriminations reveal. A question begins because something experienced calls for an account, sometimes as a clearly formulated problem, sometimes as the persistent feeling that an explanation has missed what matters, and in trying to answer we discover more of what we were asking. Whitehead's occasion likewise forms with an aim toward its own satisfaction, its inheritance entering a becoming directed toward a particular achievement, and Spanda's will is the same aim felt from within. In our epistemic use that aim stays open to change through what the inquiry meets; otherwise the satisfaction of having made an argument cohere becomes a reason to defend it against the experience it was meant to understand.

Lacan makes the relation between seeking and satisfaction more intricate still. His concept of the drive describes a circuit around an object the drive never attains, finding its satisfaction in completing the circuit, its jouissance,92 and that is how a person can stay attached to a search that keeps disappointing them, or to an answer whose defence keeps producing the conflict it promised to settle. Our question turns toward what is already present beneath the seeking. A person wants knowledge, and knowing is already occurring in the wanting, the questioning and the discovering; one answer fulfils one inquiry while the capacity through which it is enjoyed stays available for the next, so the eros of Logos can be sustained by discovery itself, without the promise that one final possession will end the need to understand. French keeps that pleasure inside its word for knowing. Savoir, to know, descends from Latin sapere, whose senses join tasting, discerning and being wise, and savour comes from its neighbour sapor, flavour.93 We can know something by bringing it into an account, and we can savour the understanding as an event we take part in: the taste arrives when an argument finally becomes clear, when sentences held apart come together and we can feel why each belongs, and it accompanies this question, this evidence, this discovery without waiting for every question to be answered. The con- of concrescence returns us to the withness of con-scire: what grows together becomes available to a knowing that can recognise its own participation in the growth.

The Processual Lens of our Meta-Epistemic Framework (L3, developed in §4) follows this course through any inquiry: what it inherits, what it makes possible, how it integrates those possibilities, and what its completion gives to another act. Once an inquiry's achieved claims can themselves become objects of further claims, self-reference returns inside the growth of knowledge, and a proof is exactly such an achieved form, something that can be identified, examined and used in another proof. In his 1931 paper, "On Formally Undecidable Propositions of Principia Mathematica and Related Systems", Kurt Gödel turned Whitehead and Russell's achievement upon itself.94 They had made proof explicit enough for every step to be checked against stated rules, and Gödel used that explicitness to make proofs into objects of arithmetic. A written proof is a finite sequence of expressions, each a string of symbols in a definite order, so by assigning numerical codes to the symbols and their arrangements Gödel could identify a whole proof by a single number from which the proof itself can be recovered; arithmetic can then examine relations among these numbers, including whether one coded expression follows from others according to the rules. Gödel gave naming and counting an exact technique: a number names an expression, and calculation with such numbers settles whether the expressions they name constitute a proof.

For this to work, we must specify what counts as a proof. Let T name the theory under consideration: its axioms state what it begins by accepting, and its rules of inference determine how further claims may be derived. We require these to be effectively specified, so that a definite procedure can check any proposed proof, and we require T to contain enough arithmetic to represent the numerical operations involved. Given a candidate proof and a proposed conclusion, we can then ask whether the candidate really establishes the conclusion according to T, and once both have codes, the question becomes a relation between numbers. We write \operatorname{Proof}_T(p,n) to mean that p codes a proof, in T, of the sentence coded by n, the subscript naming the rules under which a sequence counts as a proof. Gödel then constructs a sentence that speaks about its own provability. Substituting a numeral into an expression can itself be represented arithmetically, and by arranging the substitution he produces a sentence whose claim concerns the numerical code of that very sentence. Call it G_T, and write \ulcorner G_T\urcorner for its code. T itself can establish the equivalence:

G_T \iff \neg\exists p\,\operatorname{Proof}_T\bigl(p,\ulcorner G_T\urcorner\bigr).

In words, the sentence says that no number codes a proof, in T, of this very sentence. The corner brackets let the sentence be referred to through its numerical identity, so what it says about proving it also says about numbers, and Gödel built the passage between these two readings into the construction. Self-reference has entered through arithmetic's capacity to describe operations on the signs in which arithmetic is written.

Suppose T did prove G_T. There would then be a finite proof with a numerical code, and because T can represent the checking of proofs, it could verify that code and prove that a proof of G_T exists; yet G_T says that no such proof exists, so T would have proved a statement and its negation. Consistency, the property of never proving both a statement and its negation, excludes this, so a consistent T cannot prove G_T, and under that assumption what G_T says of the natural numbers is true: none of them codes a T-proof of it. We recognise the sentence's truth by reasoning about the theory, with the consistency on which that reasoning depends kept explicit, and the theory's inability to prove the sentence is precisely what the sentence truthfully describes. Could T instead prove the negation, that some number does code a proof of G_T? Under the consistency assumption every particular number fails the test, and T can check each failure, so proving the negation would mean denying of each number that it codes a proof while asserting that some number does. Gödel excludes this with a stronger condition he calls ω-consistency: a theory must not deny a property of each particular natural number and also assert that some natural number has it. Given that condition, T proves neither G_T nor its negation. The sentence is well formed, its meaning was built from the theory's own arithmetic, and both routes to deciding it by proof are closed under the stated assumptions; the limit becomes expressible because the theory has succeeded in representing so much of its own activity.

Russell's paradox had forced a change in the principle for forming collections, because the principle produced a contradiction. Gödel's result is of a different kind: the theory stays consistent and goes on establishing genuine truths, and still its resources cannot decide every arithmetical sentence it can formulate, so paradox returns through the success of self-reference as a separation between truth and provability within a specified theory. That puts the theory of types in its proper place, for clarifying which expressions may be formed protects reasoning from one contradiction and guarantees nothing about whether every meaningful question in the resulting theory can be settled by its proofs. Gödel's second theorem then reaches the confidence with which the theory's rules are used. Because proofs have codes, the claim that T is consistent can be written in T's own arithmetic, as the statement that no number codes a T-proof of a contradiction, and Gödel showed that for a theory strong enough to formalise the relevant reasoning, using the standard representation of its proofs, proving this consistency statement would let the theory prove its own Gödel sentence. A consistent theory cannot do that, so it cannot prove its own consistency either. Here the orientation we write 1/0 takes arithmetical form: asked to account for the condition it rests on, the determinate system can answer only with another determinate sentence in its own terms, and that sentence is precisely the one it cannot prove. Gödel does not prove our notation; within his stated hypotheses he gives the incomputable return of the 1 upon its ground a theorem's precision. Trust has become examinable at this point. Every use of the theory enacts a reliance that now appears inside the reasoning as a stated hypothesis, consistency, whose warrant the theory cannot supply, and the theory's achievements and the grounds on which we accept it have become distinguishable within the reasoning itself.

That warrant can be sought in a stronger theory, and the new work establishes results unavailable in the first, a genuine enlargement of knowledge: an independent sentence can be adopted as a new axiom, and further principles can support a proof of the earlier theory's consistency. If a stronger theory T' proves G_T, it has not contradicted G_T, because that sentence concerns the absence of a proof in T, not in T'. Gödel's construction keeps in play the three relations our counting already required: the number that makes the sentence identifiable, the proof-relation that specifies how it could be established, and the theory that decides which sequences count as establishing it. Only by keeping all three explicit can we say how one sentence stays unprovable in T while becoming provable in T', and calling it simply "unprovable" would hide the theory relative to which that holds. Once we ask T' to represent its own proving, a new sentence must refer to the enlarged proof-relation, and if T' meets the same conditions, the incompleteness result applies to it in turn. No collection of further answers repairs this, because each answer belongs to a theory with particular axioms and rules, and every enlargement produces a new relation between what the theory can express and what it can establish. The gap between those capacities is an immutable gap: it relocates with every enlargement and returns through it, so it was never one withheld fact awaiting discovery.95 Theories change, and the recurrence does not. No consistent, effectively axiomatised theory of the requisite strength can provide the complete and internally certified account of arithmetic that the original desire for certainty required, and what Gödel makes untenable is the expectation that our proving, comparing and investigating must finally produce one theory whose own resources exhaust arithmetical truth and certify their own reliability.

Nicholas of Cusa, whose infinite sphere gave us the centre everywhere and the circumference nowhere, gave On Learned Ignorance a second figure for exactly this relation: "the intellect is to truth as [an inscribed] polygon is to [the inscribing] circle. The more angles the inscribed polygon has the more similar it is to the circle. However, even if the number of its angles is increased ad infinitum, the polygon never becomes equal [to the circle] unless it is resolved into an identity with the circle."96 Each stronger theory adds sides, and the likeness genuinely grows, while equality never arrives by adding. Cusa names the only way it could arrive, a resolution into identity, which is a change in kind that no further side achieves; our square became a torus through the same kind of act, the identification of its edges, and no number of added sides would have made it one. Two limits are at work here, then, and they ground two different acts, which I hold together in four lines:

**The approachable limit is the basis of investigation;
the absolute limit is the basis of faith;
the limit of investigation is the limit of determination;
the limit of faith is never to be determined.**97

Gödel's sentence is an approachable limit: a stronger theory proves it, and investigation advances by approaching it. What reappears with every advance is an absolute limit, which no theory approaches, because each approach occurs through a further determination of the kind it would need to exceed. Investigation ends where determination ends, at the edge of the polygon it has drawn. Faith takes its basis from the circle the polygon never equals, and it is not belief in some proposition the theorem has failed to settle, since an unprovable sentence warrants no unrelated belief at all. Faith is the lucid continuation through that limit which every use of a theory already enacts, with its grounds, scope and conditions of revision retained, and the one thing it never does is turn the absolute limit into a final determination. Here the paradox of the two signs meets its formal limit: the referent of "Subject" and "God" is identified in every act of investigation and determined by none. Russell had asked investigation to supply the basis of faith. What he found, and what Gödel then made exact for arithmetic under stated hypotheses, is that such a theory cannot certify its own ground from its own resources: the two bases differ in kind, and a life of inquiry needs both.

Here the form has become articulate enough to name itself through a number and to make a claim about the possibility of its own proof, and 0/1 was introduced so that such an achieved form would never take the place of its own ground. Wittgenstein had met self-reference a decade earlier, in what a proposition must share with the world in order to represent it. A sentence can represent a possible arrangement of things because its signs have an arrangement of their own, and their relation shows what would be the case if the sentence were true; any explanation of that correspondence already uses the capacity it explains, so we cannot step outside every representation to inspect the relation between representation and reality from somewhere else. His distinction between saying and showing follows: a proposition exhibits through its articulation something it cannot add to its contents as another fact.98 The same pressure reaches the one for whom a representation is intelligible. "The subject does not belong to the world but it is a limit of the world," he writes, comparing it with the eye and its field of sight, where nothing in the field allows us to conclude that it is seen from an eye;99 an image of the eye can appear among the things seen, and adding it to the picture does not supply the seeing through which the picture becomes visible. Every reader of the Tractatus has finally to undergo this distinction in relation to the book itself, whose propositions elucidate by being recognised as senseless, so that whoever understands them climbs out through them and throws away the ladder after climbing up it.

Our tetralemma reached the same predicament by a shorter route: the definition asserted something of the Subject, met its own contradiction, and exposed the act through which assertion and denial had become possible. Both inquiries ask for more than assent to a conclusion. A person can repeat that the Subject cannot be defined while continuing to identify with whichever image of themselves they happen to prefer, and recognition occurs only when the account stops concealing the activity in which it is being understood; a proposition has served its purpose when it lets us see why another proposition cannot do everything we were asking of it. Letting silence be means allowing that recognition to remain present before we turn it into another description of what we supposedly are. Heidegger's late concept for this comportment is Gelassenheit, releasement, an old word from the German mystics and Meister Eckhart that he turned to the attitude that says yes and no to technical things at once,100 and Taoist wu wei names action freed from compulsive imposition, a notion Jung lauded in his commentary on Richard Wilhelm's translation of The Secret of the Golden Flower.101 Heidegger, Jung and the Asian traditions meet on one point: an openness beyond deliberate, manipulative mastery changes how we act, and more consequentially how we feel and know ourselves in the act. A genuinely global culture requires this reciprocity between ways of knowing, in which an encounter transforms the categories through which its participants recognise knowledge and also the quality of relation that genuine openness mints. Wisdom, Sophia, seems to be attracted by such withholding of the perfunctory and largely autonomous activity of thought's attempted grasp of the present circumstance, for openness is horizonal, the standing and circumference that constitutes a horizon as such.

The torus gives that void and silence a definite relation to the form surrounding it, whilst ensuring that the circumference bears the possibility, and drive, of uncollapsable circumambulation and circulation. On a sphere every closed loop can be shrunk continuously to a point without leaving the surface. On the torus, the two circuits we followed earlier, around the tube and around the central opening, cannot be shrunk away unless we leave the surface or change the space, and their resistance tells us something positive about the whole we traverse: the opening belongs to what makes the form a torus, and filling it would change the possible journeys. Lacan used these two circuits in his seminar on identification to separate the repeated circuits of demand around the body of the torus from the empty circle around its central hole, which stands for the object desire is organised around, since particular demands can be satisfied while that object stays out of reach.102 Our search for a final self-description runs the same circuit. Another achievement, another explanation, another person's recognition promises to settle who I am, and the next encounter finds me seeking again, because the Subject appears as a vacancy whenever I look for it among the forms through which I know myself. In 0/1 that apparent vacancy is already related to every such form as the knowing through which it appears, and the silence is felt as something missing only when a proposition is expected to occupy it.

What we seek in that vacancy is rarely an emotionally neutral definition. In Jung's concept, an archetypal image presents a relationship in a form through which it can be felt: the protecting or judging father, the abandoned child, the hero whose victory would establish his worth. Its force can become numinous, Jung's term, taken from Rudolf Otto, for a power that seizes consciousness beyond any deliberate act of choice, fascinating, commanding reverence or inspiring dread,103 and a person is drawn toward such an image because something of their life seems to be at stake in its fulfilment. The image can then become the centre of a complex, Jung's concept for a feeling-toned organisation of memories, expectations, bodily responses and interpretations, through which a delayed reply, a passing remark and an old recollection are all experienced as the same rejection, or the same promise of recognition, before the person has decided what any one of them means. In the image that organisation becomes recognisable, and its authority extends through the ways it prepares a person to receive what happens next. The theory of complex systems has a concept for this kind of repetition, the basin of attraction, a region of states into which different starting points are all drawn, and John R. Van Eenwyk's reading of Jung through chaotic dynamics treats the complex as a self-similar organisation of associations and the symbol as what carries the psyche between the known and the unknown and back.104 The complex gathers experience under a feeling-tone; a living symbol disturbs the categories through which the gathering occurs and lets a different organisation develop, which is why a symbolic event often unsettles first, changing the very terms under which the person would judge whether it is good for them. One more response inside an established pattern differs in kind from an encounter that changes the pattern itself.

The quilting point that held a household's idea of the good parent settles identities of every kind. To be "a real man", "one of the chosen", or simply "someone who knows" is to inhabit an arrangement of permissions and prohibitions, hopes and fears, and a challenge to one expression can unsettle the whole arrangement because the person's identity has been secured through it. The Name-of-the-Father binds that organisation to law and authorising speech; the imago Dei, the God-image, gives the need for ultimate authority a form in which it can be encountered. Their conjunction turns dangerous when the particular image is allowed to decide what its source must be. Then the believer's interpretation, the parent's command or the institution's judgment borrows the absoluteness of what it represents, and disagreement is received as a threat to the order of reality itself. Jung's account of the dying God-image concerns this more than any change of doctrine:105 a life must discover whether it can relinquish the form in which it has located its guarantee, and the love invested in the image explains both the pain of that relinquishment and the temptation to make somebody else bear the fault.

We write this first occlusion as Ø, the crossed zero: the zero of the Subject with the mediating stroke present but fused with the apparent subject, so that the person experiences the formed interpretation as the one who interprets, the eye mistaking a sky for its gaze. Uncrossing the zero requires the stroke to become recognisable as mediation, and erasing the stroke would erase the means through which recognition occurs.106 This gives the possibility Nietzsche pursued as self-overcoming an exact question: how can I exceed what I have become if every encounter must first confirm that this is what I am?107 The 1 that claims to have settled its own identity closes the route by which its formation could be understood differently. Yet the reality of a life belongs to the one Real, including the other lives and circumstances through which it goes on changing, and its apparent private completeness is kept up only by overlooking those relations. Recognition releases their force again, and the person becomes able to respond from more than the identity already supplied as their answer.

Différance, the differing and deferring that kept a parent's promise open to what the child would later say, presses on the supposed completeness of that answer from within language.108 No final signified stands outside this movement to make every later interpretation the repetition of a completed meaning, and even the word origin enters these relations once it is written or spoken. Derrida's criticism therefore reaches any exempt centre, including an ineffable source a theory invokes to guarantee its own account, and our notation has to be able to receive it. The written 0 is itself a determinate mark. Read as one more hidden being, it would give the self's search another object around which to organise its certainty; read as 0/1, it keeps the source inseparable from the differentiation through which it becomes expressible. Immediacy of knowing does not confer exhaustive knowledge of what is known, oneself included: I can be present to an experience while misunderstanding its meaning, its cause, or what it asks of me. Our histories of conscience and consciousness have already shown an apparently settled distinction opening under inquiry, as the words led us through shared testimony, inward witness and divine knowledge of a life before reaching their modern separation, and knowing that history changed what the words can now do. Derrida's openness can be practised in the same way, as attention to what an expression carries beyond the meaning we have fixed on it, and Wittgenstein's later account of rule-following states the matching practical requirement. Knowing how to go on cannot be secured by adding an interpretation to every application, because each interpretation must itself be understood and applied, so there has to be a way of grasping a rule that is exhibited in following it.109 We need ways of learning, correcting and acting through which a received expression can meet a circumstance it did not specify in advance, and a language for intelligence must support that capacity, or its fluency becomes one more means of defending the interpretation it began with.

Descartes's scientific inheritance carries the same question to the scale of a civilisation. His Meditations began from the certainty of experienced thinking while the existence of material things remained under examination, and in the Sixth Meditation Descartes insisted that he was not in his body as a sailor is in a ship, observing its damage from outside: pain, hunger and thirst disclosed a lived union.110 An inheritance that keeps his distinction between mind and matter, treats matter as independently certain, and then asks whether experience can be reconstructed from it has reversed the order of his inquiry, and calling that order "Cartesian" hides what was selected and what was discarded. It keeps the thinking subject as a methodological point from which the world is represented while stripping its first-person certainty of the standing Descartes gave it, and the resulting world-picture appears able to explain the knower from whose act of knowing the investigation proceeded. Adam Frank, Marcelo Gleiser and Evan Thompson call what such a reversal leaves unseen, the experience on which science depends, the blind spot of science.111 Their diagnosis places lived experience, the presence of perception and its first-person character, at the point scientific materialism relies on and fails to acknowledge as fundamental, and they separate the achievements of science from the philosophical commitments of objectivism and physicalism: a scientific model is obtained and tested through experience, while the further claim that physical reality exhausts everything is an ontological commitment, not one more experimental result. Our argument places the Subject at exactly that spot. Whatever an investigation presents as the knower becomes another determinate object, while the occurrence of knowing remains involved in presenting it, so the omission is inevitable in any account that admits only objects to its explanatory terms. An investigator can describe neural events, reported experiences and the relations between them; the structural question concerns the knowing in which those descriptions are available, including the knowing by which an explanation is recognised as successful.

Scientific materialism postpones that question by appealing to the completeness of a future physics. It admits the present account to be unfinished while granting the promised account, in advance, the power to explain everything that presently exceeds it, and the authors of The Blind Spot point out that if "physical" means whatever a future complete science will describe, its content has not yet been supplied; the expectation nevertheless governs what counts as a serious explanation now. The God-image's former office survives the rejection of its theological name. An ultimate account is expected to arrive and justify the terms under which inquiry already proceeds, so that the faith lies in the promised totality and the concealment lies in presenting that promise as the absence of metaphysics. Russell's request returns here as a culture's: investigation is asked to supply the basis of faith, and the absolute limit is treated as an approachable one not yet approached. A researcher can sincerely believe they have relinquished every such commitment while applying a criterion that admits only one kind of final answer, and the self-misrecognition is inward as well as public, because the confidence needed to pursue a programme is mistaken for knowledge that its ontology must prevail.

David Chalmers's concept of the hard problem of consciousness states the consequence as a precise distinction.112 Explaining discrimination, integration, report or behavioural control does not by itself explain why these functions are accompanied by experience, and a complete functional account can leave that question exactly where it found it. Neural correlates bring real relations between experience and physiology within reach of investigation; expecting the Subject to appear among them at last repeats the objectification our definition has already undergone, offering another identifiable process in place of what knows the process. What the governing description excludes comes back as a problem the description cannot stop generating, an epistemic return of the repressed, and here the shadow of the scientific self-image shows plainly: an inquiry devoted to evidence grows defensive toward the first-person occurrence through which evidence has any standing, because granting that occurrence a foundational role would unsettle the image of explanation to which the inquiry has become attached. Cosmology meets the observer by another door, for Brandon Carter's anthropic principle requires our expectations about the universe to take account of where and when observers like ourselves could exist, since an observation selected by the possibility of observing is not an unrestricted sample from everywhere and everywhen.113 We can make those conditions ever more explicit, and their explicitness does not place us outside them, because the cosmological account is made from within the universe whose history it describes. In both cases the investigation is strengthened when it states its position and distorted when a situated account borrows the authority of an imagined view from nowhere.

Yogic and meditative traditions already cultivate attention to the formation of experience, the movement of thought, and the difference between a conditioned self-image and the knowing through which it appears, and our Śaiva sources treat these as matters of disciplined recognition, in which changing how one attends changes what becomes available to inquiry. Their scientific reception shows a consequential asymmetry, for a meditator is welcomed as someone able to produce an unusual brain state while their understanding of the experience is treated as a cultural interpretation to be set aside before the scientific account begins, so the practice supplies material and is denied the power to change the question. Neurophenomenology proposes a different relation, in which first-person descriptions and physiological findings constrain and refine one another, and its researchers hold that cognitive neuroscience has attended mainly to the third-person side and that first-person descriptions should guide the design and analysis of physiological studies.114 Taking these sciences of consciousness seriously means letting their trained distinctions help decide what an adequate account must explain. Psychedelic inquiry makes the investigator's exposure especially palpable: in LSD and the Mind of the Universe the philosopher of religion Christopher M. Bache documents a twenty-year first-person experiment of seventy-three sessions and the long work of integrating them into ordinary life, through suffering, the loss of personal boundaries and an increasingly encompassing sense of participation in life.115 One can revise a scientific model and leave one's familiar identity undisturbed; in this kind of inquiry the habits by which one recognises oneself are exposed to transformation, and, as in the dream in which Jung killed Siegfried, his own heroic ideal, what undergoes revision includes the person judging the experience. Resistance may therefore protect something more intimate than a theoretical position. An investigator can be willing to question almost anything while refusing an inquiry in which their own settled sense of being someone becomes questionable. The arbiter thus feigns a stance of being beyond judgement or implication in the consequence of their methods, the pallid perfection of a view from nowhere; and yes, the unlocated Subject is viewing, and it is nowhere as such, and insofar as this is true it genuinely hasn't "a take" on the phenomena it takes ahold of. Mind is the one "on the take", the beneficiary and victim of its own con.

The history of discovery makes this dependence on experience hard to dismiss as a philosopher's concern. In his retrospective address of 1890 August Kekulé described approaching the structure of benzene through a dozing vision of snake-like forms, one of which seized its own tail and whirled before him; waking, he spent the rest of the night working out the hypothesis it suggested.116 The serpent that swallows its tail, the ouroboros, is among the oldest images of a form returning into itself, and here it made a closed molecular arrangement thinkable: a relation became available to intuition before its chemical implications had been articulated, and that availability gave the reasoning its direction. Tesla described a deliberate cultivation of visual imagination in which he constructed, altered and ran machines before building them, detecting their faults within the imagined construction,117 and Einstein, answering Jacques Hadamard's inquiry into mathematical thought, described visual and muscular elements he could combine before finding the words or signs to communicate their relations.118 In each case an investigator reached a relation through an image, a felt configuration or an internally enacted movement, and then gave others reasons by which the result became independently examinable; teaching those reasons need not reproduce the experience in which they were first found. Newton belongs in this history too: having studied his manuscripts, John Maynard Keynes described an intelligence whose sustained intuition preceded the proofs through which its conclusions became public, warned that the geometry of the Principia should not be mistaken for the process of its discovery, and found a Newton who inhabited a universe of divine signs, alchemical correspondences and a primordial revelation recoverable through nature and ancient texts.119 The mathematician and the interpreter of mysteries were one person, and Keynes's name for him, "the last of the magicians", challenges the later portrait that keeps the mathematics and treats the rest as an embarrassing residue.

Other encounters changed what the investigator understood inquiry to concern. Emanuel Swedenborg's scientific work was followed by dreams, visions and the conversations with angels he described as opening a sustained engagement with the spiritual world, and in his own account his earlier study of nature had prepared him for that vocation.120 Much later Federico Faggin, who led the design of the first commercial microprocessor, described an experience at Lake Tahoe in December 1990 in which he knew himself and the world as one self-knowing reality, with an overwhelming quality of love; the distinction between observer and observed changed within the experience, and its consequence was a lasting inquiry into consciousness.121 An image can disclose a structure; an experience can change what counts as a question; a transformation in the investigator can alter what an adequate explanation must include. Rational demonstration belongs within this life of discovery, alongside the intuition that gives it something to demonstrate and the experience through which its adequacy is judged. A culture misrepresents its intelligence when it inherits the articulated result and expels the event that enabled the articulation, and its blind spot then conceals more than the first-person condition of evidence: it conceals the powers through which new evidence, new forms and new questions first become available.

**The Mind of Man is just a series of failed attempts to deal with the Unknown, in the process of which, however, we deal with a lot of unknowns.**122 The failures are real, and so are the unknowns dealt with on the way: what we learn relieves suffering, exposes deceptions, corrects mistaken judgments and makes previously impossible acts available. Trouble begins with the further demand that an achievement abolish the relation on which it depends. The Greeks named the nearest thing hybris, wanton insolence, which tragedy could extend to a mortal's wish to be stronger than the gods, an overreach into an authority not its own, and in our terms it is a finite measure appropriating the authority of the unmeasurable while the institution administering it treats its own perspective as the whole.123 The paradoxes we have followed expose the cost of that demand from within the practices that make it, and their force is lost when they are taught as exceptional puzzles in the history of ideas while the inherited expectation of totality goes untouched. Epistemic humility is therefore a condition of beginning an honest inquiry, and it cannot be postponed until every confident attempt has failed. I already rely on ways of perceiving, distinguishing and recognising evidence whose justification I cannot complete before I use them, and to say "I must carry conditions and truths I cannot prove from within this account" declares that dependence at the outset, where it can still shape the design of the investigation. The opening of the torus belongs in the first drawing; it is not damage discovered after the surface has been completed. In practice this means stating what a method admits, what it brackets, and how an encounter could require those decisions to change, and the obligation holds alike for a laboratory, a political office and an artificial agent. A system that corrects its outputs while protecting every governing assumption has acquired only a limited competence in correction; humility requires the commission, the criterion and the claimed source of authority to remain examinable too.

These blind spots become political because a civilisation gives its ontology practical force through the institutions and technologies it builds. A person enters an administrative system under categories; a landscape becomes a field of resources; work becomes a quantity to be optimised; attention becomes something to predict and solicit. Each description permits real operations, and the operations alter the lives subsequently described, so everything turns on whether the people affected can change the terms under which the descriptions govern them. When only what can be rendered in the authorised account is allowed to revise that account, its administrators reproduce the closure we traced in the complex, their preferred picture supplying the criterion by which every challenge to the picture is judged, and the will to secure an advantage can pass for the defence of rational order until preserving one's position looks indistinguishable from preserving the truth. Whatever another person cannot make legible under that criterion is cancelled, (−1)+(+1)=0, as private sentiment, irrational resistance or irrelevant belief, while the advantage it protected is appropriated, (+1)−(−1)=+2, the same span counted against the dismissed as (−1)−(+1)=−2; an unfamiliar way of experiencing the world becomes a curiosity to classify, or an alien presence to fear, before it can become a reason to reconsider the classification. The denial of the Subject turns political at the moment it deprives a life of standing to question the means by which that life is administered, and what a culture lets people discover about themselves is bound up with how it expects them to live.

Across initiatory traditions the passage out of a familiar identity takes a communal form: separation from established surroundings, an interval in which ordinary assurances are suspended, and incorporation into new relationships and responsibilities. Ecstatic and psychedelic practices belong to some of these histories, their preparation and social reception helping decide what the experience becomes. Plato's divine mania returns here in its initiatory office: in the Phaedrus Socrates sets the prophetic inspiration of Delphi beside initiatory purification, poetry and the madness of love, and his own speech has to change, as what he first condemned as madness becomes something through which a person may receive more than their established understanding could provide.124 Technical accomplishment alone cannot supply the inspiration its expression serves, and adept grammatical construction doesn't equate to a transmission of wisdom. Afterwards the experience can become a story, a teaching or a ritual through which others are prepared to meet something similar, one reason why, I surmise, scripture generally leverages so powerful a form as parable and poem; this stands out in stark relief in Kurt Flasch's naming of Meister Eckhart "the philosopher of Christianity".125 Yet those expressions acquire a different authority when amnesia of the experiential origin degrades into memorising a teaching or conceptual form, and replaces actually undergoing and wrestling with what it concerns. A culture can preserve the words and images while losing the means of recognising what made them necessary, and its members inherit the resulting conventions through language, custom and the ordinary intimacy of being-with others, often long before they can tell what they have experienced from what they have learned to say. The initiatory question is whether that inheritance can expose a person to its own source, including an encounter that changes how the inheritance is understood. It doesn't help, of course, that traditional means of inducing such experiences are held under legal prohibition and treated with suspicion; superstition brandishes a rational judgement, from fear of an experience naturally overcoming of a mind's rigidity, as if hardwired networks of firing neuronal activity sought self-defence in the face of latent, potentially catalysed neuroplasticity.

Such an encounter challenges the authority of a person's account from a position the account did not authorise. Someone may have treated the boundaries of their familiar identity as the boundaries of experience itself, and what they undergo makes that identification impossible to maintain honestly; whether a more-than-human authority is at work becomes a question within the experience, instead of a proposition whose admissibility was decided beforehand. Asked in his 1959 television interview whether he believed in God, Jung answered, "I don't need to believe. I know." He gave the distinction its logic elsewhere: "Either I know a thing and then I don't need to believe it; or I believe it because I am not sure that I know it. I am well satisfied with the fact that I know experiences which I cannot avoid calling numinous or divine."126 He described a power unknown in itself that interrupted his intentions and changed the course of his life, located it in conscience, where he could argue with what exceeded his conscious will, and insisted that identifying his particular God-image with the whole of metaphysical reality would falsify what he knew; of the original behind our images, he wrote, he had no doubt, and it is inaccessible. In our terms his knowing was knowledge of an encounter, and his faith the continuation through what that knowing could not determine, the absolute limit lived as the basis of faith. This is the relation our definition asks us to keep: the experience gains authority to question the identity through which it was received, while the interpretation remains something the person must answer for, and indeed in some essential sense cannot doubt beyond. A judgment that excludes the encounter in advance cancels it, (−1)+(+1)=0, and protects the old account from examination; an interpretation that makes the encounter a warrant for its own infallibility appropriates its authority anew, (+1)−(−1)=+2, and every doubt is entered on the other side as (−1)−(+1)=−2. Deferential Intelligence begins where what has been encountered can change the means by which the next judgment is made, in a sense that echoes the traditional and often fraught notion of a covenant, or at the very least an apparently bidirectional and intentional relation (in the overdetermined senses of intent and "aboutness", philosophically speaking).

Belonging changes too: observational research associates shared psychedelic experiences with increased social connectedness, and prospective research links changes in self-experience with a stronger felt relationship to nature.127 But even having to present this as "evidence" buries the point; what connection to tradition has been severed, and what effect has this on the knower, its means of knowing, and the world it knows? Either way, these findings turn attention toward what people afterwards recognise as part of the life to which they belong, often reported to be the larger, more meaningful part. Jung's concept of individuation describes a differentiation of the person that deepens their collective relationships: he opposed any levelling of a life down to collective standards, which for him averaged out the uniqueness of the individual, while recognising that the individual's existence already presupposes relations with others, so that individuation must lead "to more intense and broader collective relationships and not to isolation".128 Insofar as that relation involves tension, influence and interdependence, this uniqueness remains a hard-won gem, such is the allure to fledgling ego of fitting in. Jung's sense of the word as becoming an in-dividual, an indivisible unity or whole, turns this into a question of wholeness: can a person acknowledge more of the relations through which they are themselves, including those their preferred identity had excluded? Becoming more fully this particular life can mean becoming less defensively separate because, paradoxically, it seems the more I know my own centre as everywhere, the more I can live with a circumference nowhere. Passing from childhood to adulthood then involves more than replacing the parent's commands with one's own. It includes discovering that the authority to act carries obligations neither the parent nor the individual simply invented, and that the question of one's own existential condition is a question native to inter-subjective living. Someone who experiences their dependence on a living earth, or some notion of transcendent dimensionality, acquires another reason to question what may be demanded in the name of private advantage or public claim. Initiatory transformation becomes consequential through such changes in attention, judgment and responsibility, but above all in the change, or perhaps utter transvaluation, of one's values.

These capacities have a sociopolitical history as well as a psychological one, and the historical importance of the drug trade for various empires supplies a geopolitical-economic one too. Hannah Arendt distinguished the solitude in which someone can think with themselves from the loneliness in which they lose a dependable relation both to others and to their own experience: isolation diminishes the capacity to act together, and loneliness leaves a person open to a logic that seems to give identity and support when no one else can.129 A person needs others through whom their experience can be tested and their identity recognised, and also enough inward freedom to question what the group tells them; destroying those relations leaves the demand for assurance intact and hands it to an authority promising the whole answer. Gustave Le Bon, writing from very different political commitments, examined how prestige lends a person, title or idea the force to command assent before its claims are examined.130 Repetition and suggestion, the method and magic of media and propaganda, let an inherited judgment feel like one's own, while distance preserves the authority it came from. Our family metaphor already showed the possibility both describe: someone can become highly competent while continuing to seek from an institution the assurance once sought from a parent, and the totalitarian societies Arendt studied revealed how far an unintelligent deference tends toward banal but definite atrocity. The capacity to perform a role can develop much further than the capacity to question why that role has become the measure of one's worth, and indeed under the command of authority, the freedom to act from one's own moral intention can become warped to the point of utter inversion.

Where adulthood is measured chiefly by earning, acquiring and remaining compliant enough to be employable and reliable enough to carry out one's given-cum-imposed responsibilities, this disparity serves an extractive economy. Our institutions reward competence while keeping recognition conditional on further performance, so that a person works to obtain what they need and also to secure permission to regard themselves as sufficient, perhaps worthy in some clear or arcane sense. Material dependence reinforces the arrangement whenever housing, food and the care of others hang on continued approval, a sword of Damocles hanging above. A society organised this way can encourage arrested development by cultivating the particular dependence it profits from: people learn to administer their own exhaustion, discipline their objections and interpret their difficulty in meeting the demand as a private failure. Hannah Arendt described a population organised into denouncing its own members, wives and husbands disowning the arrested to save their children, and prisoners made complicit in murdering their friends and fellow prisoners. Heidegger's concept of Bestand, standing-reserve, names beings, forces and nature as such becoming ordered, digitised, and packaged and stored as resource held ready for future use, effectively removed from any prior implicit participation in processes concerning anything beyond the desire for that resource.131 The same dynamic, I'd say, acquires an inward counterpart when people learn to experience their own worth chiefly through their availability. A facet of our political argument is that the meaning crisis is sustained partly through this substitution of functional recruitment for supported individuation: a person can be initiated thoroughly into the demands of an organisation while remaining unable to recognise how that organisation has acquired authority over their sense of being someone, and I sense in this a concomitant crisis of purpose. An encounter that makes the life answerable to more than its allotted role interrupts that authority; indeed being less answerable, beholden, to the demands of "this world" appears a supreme freedom, and pragmatically we catch sight of its effect, real or performed, in the charisma and magnetism of those we grant power, influential remit, to. Recognising one's belonging to other beings, a living earth and a dynamic cosmos supplies grounds for refusing demands that an employer, market or state presents as unavoidable, and such recognition threatens an extractive arrangement because it changes what the person is willing to sacrifice for its approval. More palpably, such experiences of expansive identification tend to simply dwarf the value of those making demands from a more myopic and narcissistic basis.

The meaning crisis therefore concerns how a common world-sense is organised, and a shortage of comforting stories to accompany its operation is the least of it. A civilisation cannot keep treating first-person significance as secondary to what can be extracted, measured and managed, and then expect its members to find meaning secure, or motivation and joie de vivre readily available. People learn to recognise themselves through the categories that make them useful or visible and to distrust what they cannot express in those terms. Institutions can even acknowledge the reality of inner transformation while accepting only the consequences that restore a person's usefulness: the experience is welcome if its effects become useful to an outside party. These assumptions travel through infrastructures, classifications and standards, deciding what can circulate, what receives support and which forms of knowledge may influence a decision, and their reach can become global while the worldview they come from remains partial. A genuinely global culture would have to permit more than the inclusion of other traditions as material within its own account: those traditions, and the experiences through which they understand themselves, must be able to question how the common account is made. The question of an integral epistemology is already a question of power, whether different ways of knowing can meet while keeping the capacity to alter the conditions of their meeting.

Physics itself gives reasons to doubt the picture of independent things whose relations are added afterward. In a quantum superposition the amplitudes associated with alternatives contribute to interference, and replacing their coherent relation with a classical either-or distribution changes the predictions; the distinction has observable consequences. Technical command of this relation leaves untouched how a person understands identity, authority or disagreement, so we can calculate through a non-classical formalism and still demand that a social or metaphysical question resolve into two independently complete opposing terms. Digital technology shows the same disparity, since its zeroes and ones are both determinate states, both available for discrimination and manipulation, and both belong to the 1-side of our symbolon; a culture can multiply their computational power while leaving the 0/, the unobjectifiable condition and the mediating relation, out of its understanding of itself. A civilisation built with zeroes has not thereby learned what its zero excludes. 0/1 asks for superposition to become intelligible as a relation we can consciously sustain, including where its opposed readings concern our own identities.

What we have gathered here leaves a cultural question: how can a civilisation acknowledge the limits of its knowledge while continuing to organise life, however tacitly, around the promise of a final account? People inherit more than propositions. We learn what deserves attention, whose judgment carries authority and which aspirations make a life worthwhile, and questioning those habits unsettles the world in which we learned to recognise ourselves; yet the capacities developed within that world also produce circumstances its accustomed thinking can no longer address. Decadence is an achievement defended beyond the reach of its usefulness, its powers preserved while its purposes grow harder to justify. Surviving a bust by preparing the next boom may restore the economy while leaving the household, the oikos it is supposed to sustain, less able to care for its members. Renewal would require learning from what the crisis exposes, including where the inherited measure of success helped produce it, and telling the recovery of an established order apart from a mutation in how a world is understood. Jean Gebser made that distinction his life's subject, following how consciousness becomes manifest in language, art, science and the organisation of everyday experience, and the integral advent he described is the mutation: people recognise the conditions of their own seeing and gain through that recognition a different capacity to inhabit the world they are helping to make.

#4Gebserian Diaphaneity

To paint a room in perspective, an artist must first decide where it is being seen from, and every other decision follows from that one. The far wall shrinks against the near one in exact proportion to its distance; the floor's edges converge toward a single point on the horizon; a figure's height is fixed by how far it stands from the chosen eye; colour cools and pales into the distance to carry the illusion further. Every line in the picture runs toward a point that holds nothing, the vanishing point, an empty place that nonetheless organises the whole count of the scene. These relations let the painting disclose a coherent space, and they also place its viewer: the picture is already addressed to someone standing, imaginatively, here, at the one spot from which all its lines resolve. Jean Gebser read the emergence of perspective as a mutation in how people stood toward their world. In The Ever-Present Origin he followed it through Giotto's first weighted bodies and stage-like depth, Petrarch's ascent of Mont Ventoux and his new apprehension of landscape, Alberti's construction of the visual pyramid, and Leonardo's work across painting, optics and technical invention.132 Through them the relation between artistic intuition and mathematical construction turned from symbolic placement into measured projection. On the medieval painted surface the size and placement of figures expressed their rank within a sacred story; perspective made their appearance answerable to a further relation, the distance between an object and an eye, and a flat surface became the means of constructing a coherent three-dimensional space. In essence, Mind acquired a method for plotting positions from positions: by laying out a grid, it could gather objects into a common spatial order and calculate relations that no isolated object supplied. Space became something one could work through as a matrix, so that a building could be planned, an instrument aimed and an arrangement tested before anything was built. Yet a finished picture could also conceal the position that made its precision possible, and present a view from somewhere as simply how things are. Gebser's inquiry meets our reading of Descartes at exactly this point, where doubt found the doubter already present in its own performance. Whoever looks remains involved in producing the view, while the view appears to contain everything relevant to what is seen, and our definition of the Subject explains why another picture of the observer cannot close that enclosure.

Working from inside Western metaphysics, Martin Heidegger reached the same event in "The Age of the World Picture", where he argues that the modern age is defined by a change in how beings as a whole become present: the world is no longer pictured, it is conceived and grasped as picture, and that event is one and the same as the human being becoming subiectum, the relational centre on which every being is made to depend.133 What Gebser reads in Giotto's depth and Alberti's pyramid as the perspectival fixing of a sector, Heidegger reads as Vorstellen, representing: the setting of the world before a subject who measures it. The two arrived at their diagnoses independently, and we pair them because each supplies what the other withholds: Gebser the art-historical body and an integral future, Heidegger the metaphysical mechanism and its technological consequence, the world ordered as Bestand, standing-reserve, the culture that learns to measure people by their availability. The eye at the apex of Alberti's pyramid is the knower made into a place, and our notation writes that place as Ø, the crossed zero: the zero of the Subject crossed by the stroke of its own mediation, so that the slash through which a world is seen fuses with the one who sees and the picture's means pass for its source. §1 introduces the sign and its history, and §4 follows the sequence by which the crossing comes undone. Its solution begins wherever the stroke is recognised as the mediation it is, and the zero behind it is found empty in the most generous sense: a place that holds nothing and, counted, becomes the first one from which every further count proceeds.

In Prometheus and Atlas Jason Reza Jorjani carries the same event into myth, reading technoscience through two titans.134 Prometheus is its exploratory and challenging drive: forethought, the theft of fire, the defiant provocation of whatever is given. Atlas is its world-bearing and world-picturing power, the titan who holds a world up and maps it, the condition of there being any world-picture at all. The perspectival picture is Atlas's burden, a whole world held up from one standing place, crushing whoever takes the weight for the only way a world can be carried. Prometheus is the fire that made the picture possible, the forethinking light of reason taken for human use, and Jung names forethinking Prometheus and gives it to Elijah, the old prophet whose principle his own commentary calls Logos.135 In the myth Prometheus pays for the fire chained to the rock, an eagle tearing at his liver each day, and Atlas can never set the sky down. A culture that has taken the fire and holds its world up as a picture inherits both punishments, an exposure that reopens daily and a burden it cannot rest. An integral response cannot hand the fire back, and finding a stronger titan to take the weight would only renew the sentence. Its best hope is that thought's fire consume the very notion of the world, the concept in which we have housed the separations and conflagrations of our thinking. Every culture has carried an image of the world's end, by flood, fire or judgment, and that image is live again in our fears of collapse; read notionally, the end it foretells is the end of a category, the world as one picture held up from one place. What survives the burning is worldhood, the relation in which anything becomes a world for someone, which no single picture has to contain. Atlas can then set the sky down, because nobody has to carry a whole that was never a thing, and §3's answer keeps his name: an atlas of charts, in which every local map keeps its frame visible and no single picture has to bear the whole.

Gebser came to this question as a poet and translator as well as a philosopher, through a Europe whose technical and intellectual accomplishments had failed to prevent its devastation. He left Germany in 1929, lived in Spain and then France, and reached Switzerland shortly before its borders closed in 1939, and his work among writers and artists grew through these repeated displacements. His life put to a whole civilisation the question that Russell, Gödel and Wittgenstein met at the limits of formal reason: how could people become so capable of organising their world and remain so unable to understand the forces through which they organised it? Precision in carrying out an intention gives no assurance that the intention has been examined, and a society can train its members to justify what they do while leaving untouched the image of themselves that demands the justification. Gebser looked to art because mutations surface there first, and Jung explained why they do: art, he wrote, is "constantly at work educating the spirit of the age", since the artist's unsatisfied yearning reaches back to the primordial image best fitted to compensate the one-sidedness of the present and shapes that image into a form contemporaries can receive.136 In typological terms artists lead with intuition and feeling more than with thinking. They embody and manifest a mutation before anyone formalises it as a concept, so that a new way of painting is a new way of learning to see: an intuition becomes a public object, and others develop through the encounter a capacity its maker first exercised without being able to explain it.

Sri Aurobindo pursued the transformation of consciousness from within a different political struggle. Educated in England, he became a writer and organiser for Indian independence, advocating swaraj and challenging British authority through non-cooperation and the creation of Indian institutions. Arrested in the Alipore conspiracy case in 1908, he spent a year in detention before his acquittal, and his practice of yoga deepened in prison. He moved to Pondicherry in 1910 and there developed the philosophical and spiritual work published as The Life Divine and The Synthesis of Yoga, aiming at a transformation of mind, life and body through consciousness becoming aware of its divine source. Supermind named a knowledge in which the One's differentiation into the Many proceeds through self-knowledge, so that unity never has to be reconstructed from things first assumed to exist apart. In his 1966 preface Gebser set Aurobindo's supramental beside the integral consciousness whose manifestations he had been tracing, and Ulrich Mohrhoff later developed the comparison.137 Both men asked what becomes possible when the reality through which a person knows is itself recognised in the knowing.

In a letter later discussed by Georg Feuerstein, Gebser described the question coming home to him as experience, in an awakening at Sarnath in 1961: everyday perception continued alongside extraordinary clarity, quiet jubilation and primordial trust.138 His essay "Homologies in Spiritual Attitude" described a transparency grounded in non-duality, in which concrete things remained present while the opposition separating them from the one experiencing them no longer governed the encounter, and he insisted that this was an intensification of consciousness whose realisation carried a responsibility toward humanity. What deepened in him has two Śaiva names, pratyabhijñā, recognition, and samāveśa, entry or immersion, in which what was known as a proposition about consciousness becomes the reality through which a person experiences their world. His primordial trust is the trust of the child who can ask because a language and a relation already bear the asking, the dependence already enacted in 0/1. Of the four offices we gave that dependence when the household's reliance came under question, trust, knowledge, belief and faith, Gebser met the first in its pure form at Sarnath, with no account standing between him and what sustained him: fides, the reliance already in force before any arbitration, from which credere, the renewed entrustment made once an undertaking has returned its consequences, can later be given.139 Our language of knowing is economic from the start, since we credit a report, claim a truth, give an account and assign value. Every belief and every thought draws on a credit it did not create, the reliability of language, memory, testimony and the senses, and then takes credit for the truth it borrows, presenting as its own achievement a reliance that was extended to it. Descartes met this primacy of trust when his doubt reached its inherited assurances and found itself drawing on them. Where the borrowed credit goes unacknowledged, knowledge takes the fork we watched the parent and the governing office take, turning from a support under uncertainty into a register of whom to distrust. Primordial trust reverses the direction of the account. A person recognises that the reality they had asked an explanation to guarantee was already sustaining the asking; dependence becomes directly knowable as belonging, the account is relieved of the impossible demand that it first create the ground it stands on, and credit can be extended again knowingly, as credere.

Gebser called the major shifts in how this belonging is lived mutations of consciousness, and he distinguished five structures: archaic, magical, mythical, mental and integral. They are easiest to feel through an image every reader carries, the span of a single life. An infant lives before any division of self and world. A small child lives where names and things, wishes and events, still touch. An older child lives in stories, in a drama of powers and persons. An adolescent learns to argue, to ask for reasons and to stand apart. A mature adult, at best, holds all of these at once and knows which is speaking, and an elder, at the best of old age, knows them as one life. None of the earlier layers is lost: they settle beneath the later ones, and an adult who curses a stubborn machine, falls into a story or is moved by a ritual is living from them still. Gebser's structures are this layering at the scale of a civilisation, a transpersonal and collective history, and Erich Neumann argued in the same spirit that the growing ego recapitulates a history of consciousness that myth already carries.140 The ages are long and their borders blurred: archaic participation before anything we would call history; magical life in the clan and the hunt; mythical consciousness in the oral epics and sacred kingdoms of the early civilisations; the mental structure from the Greek invention of argued philosophy to its perspectival consummation in the Renaissance; and the integral, whose signs Gebser read in the art, physics and thought of the twentieth century. Each structure extends credit to a different authority, the magical to sympathetic bonds, the mythical to the story and its gods, the mental to argued reason, and each is tempted to take credit for a truth the others helped it find. Jung's psychic functions give a third reading of the same sequence, as the Logos developing through one function after another.141 Sensation leads in the magical structure, feeling in the mythical, thinking in the mental, and intuition in the integral, where intuition holds thinking and feeling together as one capacity. Gebser's Origin is the spiritual ground of them all, and Jung had already found words for it in The Red Book: "the primordial beginning, which at the same time is what has been and what is becoming".142 The beginning is released from the merely past, originary because what issues from it goes on carrying its becoming, and Gebser's Ever-Present Origin gives that temporal paradox its cultural architecture: Origin is the source through which history takes its successive forms, never the first item on a historical line. Sought only behind us, it becomes a lost object of history; identified with the present arrangement, it leaves that arrangement no room to change; deferred to the future, it stays absent instead of being the power through which becoming happens now. Like the child, it refers two ways at once. Jung saw the child archetype as both beginning and end, "an initial and a terminal creature": it "existed before man was" and "will be when man is not."143 The first-person mystery of being born and a birthless metaphysical Origin speak through one image. In our terms Origin is the unobjectifiable Subject whose differentiation we have written as 0/1, the One that "Subject" and "God" both sign, the uncarved block that stays one through every carving.

Archaic consciousness is an undifferentiated participation in that Origin, before self, world, soul and nature are experienced as separate terms that an explanation must later bridge. Gebser called it zero-dimensional because no position yet exists from which an independent "I" surveys an external world, and the unity has not yet been recognised through the differences it contains. Archaic participation and integral recognition concern the same Origin under profoundly different conditions: what the infant lives without knowing, the mature adult has to realise through everything that has since become distinct.

In the magical structure a centre begins to emerge and to exert a will on what surrounds it, and it acts through sympathy. A name, a figure, a lock of hair or a carved image stands for its referent by sympathetic identity. It does more than represent the thing; it shares in it, so that what is done to the image is done, in some measure, to what it images. Such a bond resembles an entanglement, two things that stay joined across a distance, and its effects are psychological where they are not literal, as anyone knows who has flinched at a photograph torn in anger. Jung read the energy of this world through mana, the Melanesian word, as Codrington reported it, for an extraordinary power that is fixed in nothing and can be conveyed into almost anything: a stone, a chief, a word, a place. For Jung mana is the primitive form of the concept of psychic energy, the potency running through whatever affects a life.144 In Gebser's account ritual, incantation and trance express this participation, and he counted telepathy and clairvoyance among the capacities of a consciousness not yet governed by ordinary spatial and temporal separation. Sensation is the leading function, since the magical world is met in its raw, immediate force before any story is told about it. Magic is also the first home of the matheme, since a mark or a number that acts on what it marks is magic's own exact sign, and the formal inscriptions of later mathematics keep that efficacy in a disciplined shape. Our symbolon explains why a mark can act: the part approaches the whole through its belonging, and the act takes its force from that belonging.
